- All bodies, agencies, etc in India for health -
Body/Agency Year Established Authority Purpose/Description Key Functions/Activities Recent Statistics/Impact (2023-2025)
National Level Bodies
Ministry of Health and Family Welfare (MoHFW) 1947 Central Ministry - Objective: Formulates health policies, regulates medical education, promotes public health.
- Scope: Oversees NHM, PMJAY, ICMR; coordinates with states for UHC.
- Application: Nodal for pandemics, immunization; 2.1% GDP health spend. 		- Core Functions: Policy, funding, monitoring; departments for health, family welfare, research.
- Activities: Swachhata Hi Seva 2025; U-WIN vaccination platform launch (Sep 2024).
- Structure: Led by Minister J.P. Nadda; includes DGHS, DHR. 		₹99,858 Cr budget (2025-26, +9.78%); MMR 97, IMR 23; 1.76 lakh HWCs operational (Apr 2025).
National Medical Commission (NMC) 2020 Statutory Body (NMC Act, 2019; under MoHFW) - Objective: Regulates medical education, practice; replaces MCI.
- Scope: Standards for colleges, professionals; NEET for admissions.
- Application: Ensures quality; 1.15 lakh MBBS seats (2024-25). 		- Core Functions: Accreditation, ethics enforcement, curriculum reform.
- Activities: NEET-PG 2025; 706 new colleges approved (2024).
- Structure: 33 members; 4 autonomous boards (UG/PG, ethics). 		1.15 lakh MBBS seats (2024-25, +6.3%); 706 colleges approved (2024); NEET reforms (2025).
Medical Council of India (MCI) 1934 (Dissolved 2020) Statutory Body (Replaced by NMC) - Objective: Regulated medical education/practice pre-NMC.
- Scope: Standards, ethics; legacy influence on NMC.
- Application: Historical; now NMC successor. 		- Core Functions: Approvals, registrations (legacy).
- Activities: Dissolved; assets transferred to NMC.
- Structure: Replaced; 29 members pre-2020. 		Legacy 6 lakh doctors registered; NMC transitioned 2020-25; 2025: Full digital registry.
Indian Council of Medical Research (ICMR) 1911 Autonomous Body (under DHR, MoHFW) - Objective: Biomedical research apex body; funds/translates health research.
- Scope: Infectious diseases, NCDs, vaccines; 32 institutes.
- Application: Leads R&D for national priorities. 		- Core Functions: Extramural research, guidelines, ethics.
- Activities: Sickle cell mission (2023); AIIMS collaborations; U-WIN (2024).
- Structure: HQ Delhi; 32 ICMR institutes. 		₹2,000 Cr R&D (2025); 7 Cr screened for sickle cell (2025); 30 Cr vaccine doses exported (2023).
National Health Authority (NHA) 2017 Statutory Body (under MoHFW) - Objective: Implements PMJAY; digital health ecosystem via ABDM.
- Scope: 55 Cr beneficiaries; ABHA, registries.
- Application: UHC via insurance/digital. 		- Core Functions: PMJAY ops, ABDM; state consultations.
- Activities: ABDM 2.0 workshop (Jul 2025); Co-WIN global (2025).
- Structure: CEO-led; HQ Delhi. 		36.9 Cr cards (Mar 2025); 74 Cr ABHA; 8.5 Cr hospitalizations; 2025: Global outreach.
State Level Bodies
State Health Departments Varies (Post-1947) State Governments - Objective: Implement national health programs; state-specific services.
- Scope: Primary care, hospitals; e.g., Tamil Nadu CMCHIS.
- Application: Decentralized UHC. 		- Core Functions: NHM execution, state schemes.
- Activities: YSR Aarogyasri (AP); Mukhyamantri Amrutum (Gujarat).
- Structure: State health secretary-led; 28 states. 		1.5 Cr families under TN CMCHIS (2025); 1.2 Cr YSR cards (AP); 45 lakh Gujarat Amrutum.
Disease-Specific Agencies
National AIDS Control Organization (NACO) 1992 Under MoHFW - Objective: Prevent/control HIV/AIDS; integrate with NACP.
- Scope: Testing, ART, counseling; 2.4M PLHIV.
- Application: Reduces stigma, transmission. 		- Core Functions: ART centers, testing; NACP IV (2017-24).
- Activities: NACP V launch (2025); PrEP rollout.
- Structure: NACO HQ; 30 SACS. 		1.5M on ART (2025); 95% viral suppression; NACP V (2025-30) targets zero infections.
National Centre for Disease Control (NCDC) 1963 Under MoHFW - Objective: Public health surveillance, outbreak response.
- Scope: IDSP, zoonoses; 43 labs.
- Application: Early warning system. 		- Core Functions: Lab diagnostics, training.
- Activities: Monkeypox response (2024); AMR surveillance.
- Structure: 43 VRDLs; HQ Delhi. 		1,000 outbreaks investigated (2025); 100% VRDL coverage; 2025: AI for surveillance.
Food Safety and Standards Authority of India (FSSAI) 2006 Statutory Body (under MoF) - Objective: Regulates food safety, standards.
- Scope: FSS Act 2006; licensing, testing.
- Application: Prevents adulteration, nutrition labeling. 		- Core Functions: Standards, enforcement, research.
- Activities: FoSTaC training (2025); Eat Right Campus.
- Structure: CEO-led; 72 labs. 		1 Cr FoSTaC trained (2025); 50k inspections; 2025: AI for adulteration detection.
Program Implementation Bodies
National Health Mission (NHM) 2013 MoHFW - Objective: RMNCH+A; UHC via primary/secondary care.
- Scope: NRHM+NUHM; JSY, JSSK.
- Application: State-specific. 		- Core Functions: Free drugs, diagnostics; 60:40 funding.
- Activities: HWC upgrades; NCD screening.
- Structure: State missions; HQ Delhi. 		₹37,500 Cr (2024-25); 1.76 lakh HWCs; MMR 97, IMR 23 (2025).
WHO India 1948 WHO Country Office - Objective: Technical support for health priorities; UHC, NCDs.
- Scope: Collaborates with MoHFW; GIDH initiative.
- Application: Policy advice, capacity building. 		- Core Functions: Evidence-based guidance, monitoring.
- Activities: Global Initiative on Health Equity (Jun 2025); TB elimination support.
- Structure: Country Rep-led; 100+ staff. 		GIDH launched Jun 2025; 50 countries supported; 2025: Disability inclusion guide.
Directorate General of Health Services (DGHS) 1951 Under MoHFW - Objective: Technical support for public health, medical education.
- Scope: Hospitals, training; 30 central institutions.
- Application: Policy implementation. 		- Core Functions: Hospitals management, standards.
- Activities: Safdarjung, RML hospitals; NEET oversight.
- Structure: Director General-led. 		30 institutions (2025); 1M+ OPD (annual); 2025: Digital upgrades.
Central Drugs Standard Control Organisation (CDSCO) 1940 Under MoHFW - Objective: Regulates drugs, cosmetics, medical devices.
- Scope: Approvals, inspections; 5 zonal offices.
- Application: Ensures quality, safety. 		- Core Functions: Licensing, vigilance.
- Activities: 5,000+ approvals (2024); CDSCO app.
- Structure: DCG-led; 5 zones. 		5k approvals (2024); 2025: AI for drug monitoring.
- All policies, acts, welfare schemes, etc in India for education -
Act/Policy/Scheme Year Authority Purpose/Description Key Provisions Recent Statistics/Impact (2023-2025)
Constitutional Provisions
Right to Education (RTE) Act 2009 Ministry of Education - Objective: Ensures free and compulsory education for children aged 6-14 years; implements Article 21A.
- Scope: Neighborhood schools, 25% reservation in private schools for disadvantaged.
- Application: Promotes equity, quality in elementary education. 		- Provisions: No detention till Class 8 (amended 2019); teacher norms, infrastructure standards (Sections 3-18).
- Implementation: Monitored via UDISE+; state surveys. 		96% elementary enrollment (2024); 3.05 lakh applications in Maharashtra (2025); Gujarat admissions until Mar 2025; 25% quota filled 88% (2024-25).
RTE Act Amendment 2017 Ministry of Education - Objective: Removes no-detention policy for Classes 5 & 8; introduces regular exams.
- Scope: Applies to elementary schools; allows retention with remediation.
- Application: Improves learning outcomes. 		- Provisions: Re-exam within 2 months; retention if failed; aligns with NEP.
- Implementation: State boards; NCPCR monitoring. 		Implemented in 20+ states (2025); retention up 5%; 91% surveyed students retained (2025 Indus Action).
National Policies
National Education Policy (NEP) 2020 Ministry of Education - Objective: Transform education; holistic, flexible, multidisciplinary learning.
- Scope: ECCE to higher education; 5+3+3+4 structure, GER 50% by 2035.
- Application: Guides Samagra Shiksha, ECCE, HEFA. 		- Provisions: Multilingualism, vocational education; board exam reforms.
- Implementation: State NEP cells; NIPUN Bharat linkage. 		30+ states implemented (2025); GER 28.4% (2024); 4-year UG degree in 5,000+ colleges (2025).
Early Childhood Care and Education (ECCE) 2021 Ministry of Education - Objective: Universal access to quality ECCE for ages 3-6 years; foundational learning.
- Scope: Anganwadis, pre-primary classes; play-based curriculum.
- Application: Aligns with NEP; reduces stunting. 		- Provisions: Curriculum framework; teacher training; 90% enrollment target.
- Implementation: Anganwadis, Samagra Shiksha. 		1.4 Cr anganwadis (2025); 85% ECCE coverage; 2025: 1 lakh pre-primary sections added.
National Mission on Foundational Literacy and Numeracy 2021 Ministry of Education - Objective: Achieve universal FLN by Grade 3 by 2026-27.
- Scope: Grades 1-3; teacher training, assessments.
- Application: Part of NIPUN Bharat. 		- Provisions: FLN tools, Vidya Pravesh; state missions.
- Implementation: DIKSHA, UDISE+. 		30+ state missions (2025); 80% Grade 3 FLN target met; 2025: Digital tools scaled.
School Education Schemes
Samagra Shiksha Abhiyan 2018 Ministry of Education - Objective: Integrated school education from pre-school to Class 12.
- Scope: Subsumes SSA, RMSA, TE; equity, quality focus.
- Application: Aligns with NEP; funds infrastructure, training. 		- Provisions: Free textbooks, uniforms; teacher training; 60:40 funding.
- Implementation: State education boards. 		₹37,383 Cr (2024-25); 95% schools with toilets; 1 Cr teachers trained (2025).
Mid-Day Meal (PM-POSHAN) Scheme 1995 (Revamped 2021) Ministry of Education - Objective: Nutritional support to enhance enrollment, retention.
- Scope: Classes 1-8 in govt/aided schools; 11.8 Cr children.
- Application: Aligns with NFSA, NEP. 		- Provisions: 450-700 kcal meals; ₹10,000 Cr budget.
- Implementation: State nodal agencies; Akshaya Patra. 		11.8 Cr children (2025); dropout rate down 2%; 2025: Millets added to menu.
NIPUN Bharat Mission 2021 Ministry of Education - Objective: Universal foundational literacy/numeracy by 2026-27.
- Scope: Grades 1-3; curriculum, teacher support.
- Application: NEP-aligned; DIKSHA integration. 		- Provisions: FLN tools, assessments; state FLN cells.
- Implementation: NCERT, CBSE support. 		30+ states with FLN cells (2025); 5 Cr students assessed; 2025: 80% literacy target.
Scheme for Providing Quality Education in Madrasas (SPQEM) 2009 Ministry of Education - Objective: Modern education in madrasas; mainstream subjects.
- Scope: Maths, science, English; teacher salaries.
- Application: Targets minority students. 		- Provisions: ₹50,000/madrasa; DEd/BEd support.
- Implementation: State madrasa boards. 		10,000 madrasas (2025); 5 lakh students; 2025: Digital content via DIKSHA.
Scheme for Providing Education to Madrasas/Minorities (SPEMM) 2009 Ministry of Education - Objective: Infrastructure, quality education for minorities.
- Scope: Madrasas, minority schools; vocational focus.
- Application: Complements SPQEM. 		- Provisions: Grants for labs, libraries; teacher training.
- Implementation: State minority departments. 		2 lakh students (2025); 1,000 madrasas upgraded; 2025: Skill labs added.
Digital Education Initiatives
DIKSHA (Digital Infrastructure for Knowledge Sharing) 2017 Ministry of Education - Objective: Digital platform for e-content, teacher training.
- Scope: Classes 1-12; open-access resources.
- Application: NEP-aligned; NIPUN Bharat support. 		- Provisions: 5M+ resources; multilingual content.
- Implementation: NCERT, state boards. 		100M+ learners (2025); 50,000 e-contents added; 2025: AI personalization launched.
Prashikshak Portal 2016 Ministry of Education - Objective: Monitor, improve teacher training quality.
- Scope: DElEd/BEd institutes; performance tracking.
- Application: Supports Samagra Shiksha. 		- Provisions: Real-time data; trainer profiles.
- Implementation: NCTE-managed. 		5,000 institutes tracked (2025); 1 Cr teachers trained; 2025: Integrated with DIKSHA.
e-Adhigam 2022 Ministry of Education - Objective: Digital tablets for personalized learning.
- Scope: Govt schools; content via DIKSHA.
- Application: NEP digital thrust. 		- Provisions: Tablets with pre-loaded content; state funding.
- Implementation: State education depts. 		10 lakh tablets distributed (2025); Haryana led with 5 lakh; 2025: AI analytics added.

- Implementation: DST-managed. 		₹2,000 Cr disbursed (2025); 1,000 projects funded; 2025: Industry linkages scaled.

- All bodies, agencies, etc in India for education -
Body/Agency Year Established Authority Purpose/Description Key Functions/Activities Recent Statistics/Impact (2023-2025)
National Level Bodies
Ministry of Education (MoE) 1947 Central Ministry - Objective: Formulates and implements education policies; oversees school, higher, skill development.
- Scope: Coordinates NEP 2020, Samagra Shiksha, higher education expansion.
- Application: Nodal for UHC in education; GER 50% target by 2035. 		- Core Functions: Policy, funding, monitoring; departments for school/higher education.
- Activities: NEP implementation reviews; ₹1,12,000 Cr budget (2023-24); Swachhata Hi Seva 2025.
- Structure: Led by Minister Dharmendra Pradhan; includes DHE, DSDE. 		₹1,20,628 Cr allocation (2024-25); GER 28.4% (2024); 30+ states NEP-aligned (2025).
Department of Higher Education (DHE) 1947 Under MoE - Objective: Promotes higher education access, quality; oversees universities, colleges.
- Scope: UGC, AICTE, NIRF; HEIs expansion.
- Application: Implements NEP higher education reforms. 		- Core Functions: Grants, accreditation, research funding.
- Activities: PM-USHA for infrastructure; 706 new colleges approved (2024).
- Structure: Secretary-led; coordinates UGC, AICTE. 		₹47,619 Cr (2024-25); 1.15 lakh MBBS seats (2024-25); 20 IoEs funded (2025).
University Grants Commission (UGC) 1956 Statutory Body (Under DHE) - Objective: Coordinates, maintains standards in universities; funds HEIs.
- Scope: Grants, regulations, accreditation.
- Application: NEP-aligned; 1,000+ universities. 		- Core Functions: University recognition, scholarships, quality assurance.
- Activities: SWAYAM MOOCs; 4-year UG programs (2025).
- Structure: Chairman-led; 22 members. 		₹4,600 Cr grants (2024-25); 5,000+ HEIs regulated; 2025: Hybrid learning guidelines.
Central Advisory Board of Education (CABE) 1935 Advisory Body (Under MoE) - Objective: Advises central/state govts on education policy.
- Scope: School/higher education; 45 members.
- Application: NEP formulation, reviews. 		- Core Functions: Recommendations, consultations.
- Activities: NEP sub-groups (2021-25); annual meetings.
- Structure: Chaired by Education Minister. 		Revived 2021; 10 sub-groups for NEP (2025); influenced 30+ state policies.
National Board of Accreditation (NBA) 1994 Autonomous Body (Under AICTE) - Objective: Accredits technical programs for quality.
- Scope: Engineering, management; international recognition.
- Application: Washington Accord signatory. 		- Core Functions: Program evaluation, certification.
- Activities: 2025 accreditation cycle; 1,000+ programs.
- Structure: Board with experts. 		1,200 programs accredited (2025); 80% engineering colleges compliant; 2025: AI programs included.
National Assessment and Accreditation Council (NAAC) 1994 Autonomous Body (Under UGC) - Objective: Assesses/accredits HEIs for quality improvement.
- Scope: Universities, colleges; graded accreditation.
- Application: NEP binary accreditation. 		- Core Functions: Peer team visits, grading (A++ to C).
- Activities: 2025 overhaul for data-driven; binary system pilot.
- Structure: HQ Bengaluru; 820 universities accredited (2023). 		820 universities, 15,501 colleges accredited (Jun 2023); 2025: Corruption probe led to 5-year debarment of 1 HEI.
National Testing Agency (NTA) 2017 Autonomous Body (Under MoE) - Objective: Conducts entrance exams for higher education.
- Scope: NEET, JEE, CUET, UGC-NET.
- Application: Standardizes admissions. 		- Core Functions: Exam conduct, evaluation.
- Activities: NEET-UG 2025 (May 2025); CUET twice yearly.
- Structure: HQ Delhi; 2 Cr candidates/year. 		2 Cr candidates (2025); NEET-PG 2025 conducted; 2025: Reforms post-paper leak probe.
Anusandhan National Research Foundation (NRF) 2023 Ministry of Education - Objective: Fund research in sciences, humanities; ₹50,000 Cr corpus.
- Scope: Replaces SERB; inter-disciplinary.
- Application: NEP research goal. 		- Core Functions: Grants, fellowships.
- Activities: ₹2,000 Cr disbursed; 1,000 projects.
- Structure: DST-managed. 		₹14,000 Cr budget (2025); 1,000 projects funded; 2025: Industry linkages.
Science and Engineering Research Board (SERB) 2008 Department of Science and Technology - Objective: Fund basic research in sciences/engineering.
- Scope: Grants, core research groups.
- Application: Precursor to NRF. 		- Core Functions: Project funding; ₹1,000 Cr annual.
- Activities: 5,000 projects (2023); transitioned to NRF.
- Structure: Board-led. 		5,000 projects (2023); legacy grants continued (2025); 2025: AI research focus.
Higher Education Financing Agency (HEFA) 2017 Ministry of Education - Objective: Fund infrastructure in premier HEIs via loans.
- Scope: IITs, NITs; ₹1 lakh Cr target.
- Application: NEP infrastructure. 		- Core Functions: Low-interest loans.
- Activities: ₹20,000 Cr disbursed; 100 HEIs.
- Structure: Canara Bank JV. 		₹20,000 Cr loans (2025); 100 HEIs funded; 2025: IoE expansion.

- All policies, acts, welfare schemes, etc in India for poverty, employment, and nutrition -
Act/Policy/Scheme Year Authority Purpose/Description Key Provisions Recent Statistics/Impact (2023-2025)
International Guidance
Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) – International Guidance 1945 United Nations Specialized Agency - Objective: Provides global standards for food security, nutrition; supports India's poverty/nutrition policies.
- Scope: SDG 2 (Zero Hunger); technical aid for MFP, climate-resilient agriculture.
- Application: Influences NFSA, POSHAN; aligns with UN Decade of Action on Nutrition (2016-2025). 		- Provisions: Guidelines for sustainable food systems; capacity building in 150+ countries.
- Implementation: Partnerships with MoA, MoRD; annual SOFI reports. 		India's extreme poverty fell to 2.3% (2022-23, World Bank); FAO aided POSHAN in 10 Cr beneficiaries; 2025 SOFI highlights India's 171M lifted from poverty (2011-2023).
Poverty Alleviation Schemes
Public Distribution System (PDS) 1960s Ministry of Consumer Affairs, Food and Public Distribution - Objective: Affordable food distribution to poor; stabilizes prices.
- Scope: Essential commodities; 80 Cr beneficiaries.
- Application: Targets BPL; integrates with NFSA. 		- Provisions: Subsidized grains (₹1-3/kg); Aadhaar-linked; One Nation One Ration Card (ONORC).
- Implementation: State FCSCs; FCI procurement. 		80 Cr beneficiaries (2025); ₹2.5 lakh Cr savings; ONORC used by 100 Cr (2025).
Targeted Public Distribution System (TPDS) 1997 Ministry of Consumer Affairs, Food and Public Distribution - Objective: Targeted PDS for BPL; prevents diversion.
- Scope: APL/BPL; 5 kg/person/month.
- Application: Reduces leakages; data-driven targeting. 		- Provisions: SECC 2011 data; digital allocation.
- Implementation: State portals; DBT. 		81 Cr BPL cards (2025); leakages down 50%; 2025: AI for fraud detection.
National Food Security Act (NFSA) 2013 Ministry of Consumer Affairs, Food and Public Distribution - Objective: Legal right to subsidized food; nutritional security for 67% population.
- Scope: 5 kg grains/person/month; maternity entitlements.
- Application: PDS backbone; covers 81 Cr. 		- Provisions: Priority households; grievance redressal (Sections 3-12).
- Implementation: State PDS; Aadhaar. 		80 Cr beneficiaries (2025); ₹2.5 lakh Cr savings; 2025: Integrated with POSHAN 2.0.
Pradhan Mantri Garib Kalyan Anna Yojana (PMGKAY) 2020 Ministry of Consumer Affairs, Food and Public Distribution - Objective: Free 5 kg grains + 1 kg dal/month to NFSA beneficiaries; COVID relief.
- Scope: 81 Cr people; extended annually.
- Application: Enhances NFSA during crises. 		- Provisions: 100% free; DBT; ONORC portability.
- Implementation: FCI; state warehouses. 		₹11.8 lakh Cr (2020-25); 81 Cr fed monthly; extended to 2029 (2025).
Pradhan Mantri Awas Yojana (PMAY) 2015 Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs (PMAY-U) / Rural Development (PMAY-G) - Objective: Affordable housing for all; poverty reduction via shelter.
- Scope: PMAY-G (4.2 Cr houses); PMAY-U (1.2 Cr).
- Application: Housing for EWS/LIG; rural/urban. 		- Provisions: ₹1.2-2.5 lakh subsidy; CLSS for loans.
- Implementation: HUDCO, state agencies. 		4.21 Cr houses sanctioned (2025); 1.19 Cr PMAY-U completed; 2025: 2 Cr more targeted.
Swachh Bharat Mission (SBM) 2014 Ministry of Jal Shakti (SBM-G) / Housing and Urban Affairs (SBM-U) - Objective: Sanitation, cleanliness to reduce poverty-related health risks.
- Scope: 11 Cr toilets; ODF India.
- Application: Rural/urban hygiene; SDG 6. 		- Provisions: ₹12,000/toilet subsidy; waste management.
- Implementation: State missions; Swachh Survekshan. 		11 Cr toilets built (2025); 100% ODF villages; 2025: SBM 2.0 waste focus.
Saubhagya Scheme (Household Electrification) 2017 Ministry of Power - Objective: Universal electricity access; poverty alleviation.
- Scope: 2.86 Cr households electrified.
- Application: Rural/urban poor; SDG 7. 		- Provisions: Free connections; ₹16,320 Cr budget.
- Implementation: REC, state discoms. 		2.86 Cr households electrified (2023); 100% village electrification; 2025: Solar integration.
Deen Dayal Antyodaya Yojana (DAY-NRLM) 2011 Ministry of Rural Development - Objective: Poverty reduction via SHGs, livelihoods.
- Scope: 10 Cr rural women; SHG mobilization.
- Application: Rural self-employment. 		- Provisions: ₹10,000 Cr; microfinance, skill training.
- Implementation: SRLMs; banks. 		10 Cr women in 83 lakh SHGs (2025); ₹2.65 lakh Cr credit; 2025: Lakhpati Didi scaled.
NITI Aayog’s Multidimensional Poverty Index (MPI) 2021 NITI Aayog - Objective: Measure poverty beyond income; health, education, living standards.
- Scope: 12 indicators; NFHS-5 data.
- Application: Informs PMAY, SBM, JJM targeting. 		- Provisions: Deprivation scoring; district-level data.
- Implementation: NFHS-based; state reports. 		14.96% MPI poor (2022-23); 171M lifted (2011-23); 2025: Digital MPI dashboard.
Pradhan Mantri Jan Dhan Yojana (PMJDY) 2014 Ministry of Finance - Objective: Financial inclusion for poor; banking access.
- Scope: Zero-balance accounts; 50 Cr beneficiaries.
- Application: Enables DBT for schemes. 		- Provisions: RuPay cards; ₹2 lakh insurance.
- Implementation: Banks; DFS. 		50 Cr accounts (2025); ₹2.1 lakh Cr deposits; 2025: Digital banking scaled.
Lakhpati Didi 2023 Ministry of Rural Development - Objective: Empower SHG women to earn ₹1 lakh/year.
- Scope: 3 Cr women; DAY-NRLM linkage.
- Application: Women-led poverty alleviation. 		- Provisions: Skill training, microfinance.
- Implementation: SRLMs; banks. 		1 Cr women empowered (2025); 3 Cr target by 2027; 2025: Drone Didi expansion.

National Rural Employment Guarantee Act (MGNREGA) 2005 Ministry of Rural Development - Objective: 100 days guaranteed wage employment to rural households.
- Scope: Unskilled manual work; 13 Cr households.

- Implementation: Gram panchayats; MIS portal. 		13 Cr households (2025); ₹86,000 Cr budget; 2025: 6 Cr person-days generated.
Pradhan Mantri Kaushal Vikas Yojana (PMKVY) 2015 Ministry of Skill Development and Entrepreneurship - Objective: Skill training for employability; industry-aligned courses.
- Scope: Short-term training, RPL; 1.63 Cr trained.
- Application: NSDM component. 		- Provisions: Free training; ₹12,000 Cr budget.
- Implementation: NSDC, SSCs. 		1.63 Cr trained (2015-25); 70% placement; 2025: PMKVY 4.0 launched.
Stand Up India 2016 Ministry of Finance - Objective: Promote entrepreneurship among SC/ST, women.
- Scope: ₹10 lakh-1 Cr loans; 2.5 lakh beneficiaries.
- Application: MSME support. 		- Provisions: Collateral-free loans; SIDBI-led.
- Implementation: Banks, SIDBI. 		2.5 lakh loans (2025); 80% women; 2025: Green startups focus.
Prime Minister’s Employment Generation Programme (PMEGP) 2008 Ministry of MSME - Objective: Micro-enterprise creation for employment.

- Implementation: Banks, DICs. 		1.4M jobs (2025); ₹12,000 Cr disbursed; 2025: Digital application portal.
Employment-Linked Incentive (ELI) Scheme 2025 Ministry of Labour and Employment - Objective: Generate 3.5 Cr jobs; formalize employment.
- Scope: MSMEs, startups; wage subsidies.

Pradhan Mantri Street Vendor’s AtmaNirbhar Nidhi (PM SVANidhi) 2020 Ministry of Housing and Urban Affairs - Objective: Credit for street vendors; urban poverty alleviation.

POSHAN Abhiyaan 2018 Ministry of Women and Child Development - Objective: Reduce malnutrition, stunting, anemia in children, women.

Annapurna Scheme 2000 Ministry of Consumer Affairs, Food and Public Distribution - Objective: Food security for uncovered elderly; poverty alleviation.

- Scope: ₹5-8 meals; mobile vans.
- Application: Nutrition for laborers. 		- Provisions: 500+ vans; state subsidy.
- Implementation: State nodal agency. 		500 vans (2025); 2 lakh meals/day; 2025: Expanded to rural areas.

- Current affairs -
- UNIT 1- VULNERABLE SECTIONS AND WELFARE SCHEMES FOR THEIR PROTECTION - 1. Vulnerable Sections in India - - Vulnerable sections refer to groups of people who face higher risks and disadvantages due to various socio-economic, cultural, and environmental factors. These populations experience systemic exclusion, marginalization, and inequitable access to resources, rights, and opportunities. - Vulnerability is often influenced by the intersectionality of factors such as economic status, gender, caste, disability, age, and ethnicity. Vulnerable groups typically lack the power to influence social, political, and economic decisions and are subject to discrimination, neglect, or abuse. - Rationale of Welfare Schemes for Vulnerable Sections - Constitutional Basis - The Preamble of the Constitution promotes justice, equality, and dignity. - Fundamental Rights protect and promote the idea of equality, the right to live with dignity, and rights against untouchability and exploitation. - Directive Principles emphasize the state’s responsibility for citizens' welfare, helping India become a Welfare State. - Economic Reasons - The Idea of Inclusive growth is considered vital for sustained economic progress of any nation. - Welfare schemes for vulnerable sections help reduce poverty, support the labor force, and facilitate active participation of Vulnerables in economic development. - Nation Building - Excluding vulnerable groups leads to social resentment and inequality, hindering nation-building efforts. - Ensuring equitable access improves societal harmony, fostering a sense of unity and inclusion. - 1.1. Women in India - "When one woman is empowered, she becomes a catalyst for change" - Michelle Bachelet (Former Chilean President) - Women in India are recognized as a vulnerable section of the population, facing systemic disadvantages due to socio-cultural, economic, and political factors. This vulnerability is often structured, stemming from imperfect or unjust societal systems. Women specifically face significant barriers in terms of access to education, economic independence, legal justice, and healthcare. - Key Data Regarding Women's Vulnerabilities - Health & Social Vulnerabilities - Child Marriage (2019–21): - 23.3% of women aged 20–24 were married before reaching the legal age of 18. - Sex Ratio at Birth (2018–20): - The ratio remains unfavorably low at 907 female births for every 1,000 male births, pointing to the persistence of son preference. - Maternal Mortality (2018–20): - The Maternal Mortality Ratio (MMR) stood at 97 deaths per 100,000 live births, compared to the SDG benchmark of 70. - Malnutrition (2019–21): - 18.7% of women between the ages of 15–49 have a Body Mass Index (BMI) that is below normal. - Health Awareness (2019–21): - Only 21.6% of women have comprehensive knowledge of HIV/AIDS. - Economic Vulnerabilities - Labour Force Participation (2023–24): - The Labour Force Participation Rate for females aged 15 and above is only 41.7%. - Wage Gap (Jan–Mar 2024): In casual labour, women earned significantly less than men. - Rural: - Women earned an average of ₹286 per day compared to men's ₹407. - Urban: - Women earned an average of ₹358 per day compared to men's ₹529. - Burden of Unpaid Work (2024): - On an average day, female participants spend 330 minutes on unpaid domestic services, while male participants spend only 101 minutes on the same. - Educational Disadvantages - Gender Gap in Literacy (2017–18): - A significant literacy gap of 14.4 percentage points persists between men and women aged 7 and above. - Dropout Rate (2023–24): - The dropout rate for girls increases significantly with the level of education, reaching 12.60% at the secondary level (Class IX–X). - Crime and Violence - Domestic Violence (2019–21): - 29.3% of ever-married women aged 18–49 have experienced physical, sexual, or emotional violence committed by their husband. - Major Crimes (2022): - "Cruelty by Husband or his Relatives" was the most reported major crime against women, with 140,546 cases filed. - Rape and Cyber Crimes (2022): - There were 31,516 reported victims of rape. 15,969 cases of cybercrimes targeting women were reported. - 1.1.1. Key Challenges Faced by Women in India - Deep-Rooted Patriarchy & Social Discrimination - From Birth to Adulthood: - Discrimination starts early—female foeticide, child marriage, and dowry practices reinforce inequality. - Restricted Roles: - Patriarchal norms confine women to household duties, limiting education and career growth. - Stigma & Shame: - Menstruation remains taboo, forcing many girls to drop out of school, while working women lack menstrual leave policies. - Honor & Control: - Practices like honor killings and acid attacks suppress women’s freedom under the guise of "family reputation." - Violence & Exploitation – A Daily Battle - Rising Crimes: - 87 rape cases are reported daily (NCRB), and domestic abuse remains widespread. - Unsafe Public Spaces: - Fear of harassment restricts mobility and independence. - Economic Inequality – The Invisible Struggle - Low Workforce Participation: - Only 36.9% of women are in formal jobs (2025), far below global averages. - Gender Pay Gap: - Women earn just 39.8% of men’s wages for the same work. Most working women are in low-paying, unstable jobs with no social security. - Ownership Trends Image: - - Unpaid Labor Burden: - Women spend 5.6 hours daily on unpaid care work—equivalent to an invisible tax, draining their potential. - Unequal Ownership of Assets: - Women in India consistently have lower ownership levels than men across key assets, including houses (42.3% for women vs. 62.5% for men), land (31.7% vs. 43.9%), mobile phones (54% vs. 91%), and bank accounts (79% vs. 86%). - Health & Nutrition – Ignored & Underserved - Anemia & Malnutrition: - Half of Indian women suffer from anemia (NFHS-5). - Maternal Health Risks: - High maternal mortality and lack of proper healthcare endanger mothers. - Cancer & Neglect: - Cervical cancer (2nd most common among women) lacks awareness and HPV vaccine access. - Cervical Cancer Statistics Image: - - Aging Without Care: - Widows and elderly women face neglect, with little support for their health needs. - Lack of Representation & Power - Political Marginalization: - Only 14.4% of Lok Sabha MPs are women. - Sarpanch Pati Culture: - Often husbands take oaths for elected women leaders, mocking grassroots empowerment. - Limited Access to Rights: - Many women lack property ownership, legal aid, and digital literacy, keeping them dependent. - Limited Access to Rights: - Many women lack property ownership, legal aid, and digital literacy, keeping them dependent. - Side note - The Shadow Pandemic- Gender Violence and Mental Health Crisis - The Shadow Pandemic refers to the rise in gender-based violence (GBV), especially domestic violence, during the COVID-19 lockdowns (UN Women). - Factors like isolation, economic stress, and social unrest worsened violence, impacting women, children, and vulnerable groups. - Simultaneously, mental health issues like stress, depression, and anxiety increased due to the lockdown. - Policy responses included emergency helplines, shelters, and mental health services to support affected individuals. - Invisible Labor- The Unacknowledged Tax - The "Invisible Labor Tax" highlights the disproportionate burden of unpaid care work on women. - This unacknowledged "tax" consumes time and energy, limiting women’s economic participation and well-being. - Addressing this requires re-engineering societal structures. Investing in universal care infrastructure (like childcare) and promoting shared responsibilities can reduce this burden. - Support for Working Women and Survivors of Violence - Sakhi Niwas: - Provides safe, affordable housing for working women and job seekers. These hostels offer daycare facilities for children, helping women maintain economic independence. States receive financial aid to run these centers. - Sakhi Centres (OSCs): - Over 800 operational centres provide essential services like medical, legal, and psycho-social support to women affected by violence. These centres have assisted over 1.1 million beneficiaries, offering crucial relief and safety. - 1.1.2. Government Schemes and Policies for Women's Protection and Empowerment - Constitutional Safeguards - Fundamental Rights (Articles 14, 15, 21, 23): - Guarantee equality, protection from discrimination, and the right to live with dignity. - Article 42 and 73rd Constitutional Amendment (Article 243D): - Article 42 ensures humane conditions for women in workplaces. Article 243D mandates one-third reservation for women in Panchayati Raj Institutions. - Legislative Interventions - Prohibition of Child Marriage Act (2006): - Aims to protect women from child marriage. - Domestic Violence Act (2005): - Aims to protect women from domestic violence. - Sexual Harassment of Women at Workplace Act (2013) and Maternity Benefit Act (2017): - Sexual Harassment Act mandates mechanisms to combat workplace harassment. Maternity Benefit Act provides 26 weeks of paid maternity leave. - Welfare Schemes for Girls - Beti Bachao Beti Padhao (BBBP): - Addresses gender-biased sex selection and promotes girls' education. - Sukanya Samriddhi Yojna: - Encourages saving for girls' education and marriage. - Udaan Scheme: - Provides scholarships for girls from minority communities for higher education. - Welfare Schemes for Women's Safety - One Stop Centre Scheme: - Provides support to women affected by violence. - Swadhar and Short Stay Homes: - Offer shelter and rehabilitation for destitute women. - Nirbhaya Fund: - Finances initiatives for women's safety and dignity. - Economic Empowerment of Women - Pradhan Mantri Mudra Yojana (PMMY): - Facilitates collateral-free loans for women entrepreneurs. - Deendayal Antyodaya Yojana: - Empowers rural women through Self-Help Groups (SHGs). - Rashtriya Mahila Kosh (RMK): - Provides micro-financing to poor women for income-generating activities. - Stand Up India Scheme: - Supports SC/ST and women entrepreneurs with bank loans for greenfield enterprises. - Health and Social Security - Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (PMJAY): - Provides health insurance to vulnerable women, including Anganwadi workers and their families. - Pradhan Mantri Ujjwala Yojana: - Ensures free LPG connections for BPL women to safeguard their health and well-being. - - Nari Shakti Vandan Adhiniyam (Women's Reservation Act) - - The Nari Shakti Vandan Adhiniyam, passed in 2023, is a landmark constitutional amendment aimed at improving women’s participation in Indian politics. - It mandates the reservation of one-third of seats for women in the Lok Sabha, State Legislative Assemblies, and the Delhi Assembly. - Though hailed as a long-pending reform, its full implementation depends on future census and delimitation exercises. - Implementation Challenges - The rotational nature of reserved seats may discourage long-term investment by elected women. - There is also concern over proxy representation by male relatives, limited inclusion of OBC and minority women, and lack of broader electoral reforms to support genuine empowerment. - Learning from an Example- Women Empowerment in Norway - Norway offers a powerful case study in using policy reform to accelerate women’s representation in leadership. In 2003, it mandated a 40 percent quota for women on corporate boards, raising female representation from just 9 percent to over 40 percent in a few years. This is a clear example of how strong legal mandates can drive visible change. - However, the move also revealed a crucial lesson. While quotas can open doors at the top, they do not automatically dismantle deeper structural and cultural barriers that prevent women from becoming CEOs or holding key executive roles. - Key takeaway- True empowerment requires a two-pronged approach. - First, there must be affirmative policies to ensure women get a seat at the table. - Second, there must be long-term investment in equality of opportunity through mentorship, skill development, inclusive work cultures, and access to leadership pipelines. Empowerment must reach every level, not just the boardroom. - 1.2. Children - "Every child comes with the message that God is not yet discouraged of man" - Rabindranath Tagore - In India, children are defined as individuals under the age of 18 years, unless they reach adulthood earlier by applicable law. Children account for 39% of the population of India according to Census 2011. - Children represent one of the most vulnerable sections of society, and are highly susceptible to exploitation, abuse, and neglect. - Their vulnerability is further heightened in difficult circumstances such as being orphans, refugees, child laborers, or victims of abuse. - 1.2.1. Key Challenges Faced by Children - - Education - Learning Gaps and Dropout Rates: - Malnutrition and inadequate early childhood education contribute to learning gaps. 1.72 crore children dropped out, and school enrolment fell from 26.5 crore in 2021-22 to 24.8 crore in 2023-24. - Access to Pre-Primary Education: - Children from economically weaker sections are less likely to access pre-primary education, which impedes their learning journey. - Best Practices: - Shala Praveshotsav - Gujarat's annual school enrollment drive has reduced dropout rates from 35% in 2003 to just 0.85% in 2024, enrolling children in kindergarten and Classes 1, 9, and 11. - Thalliki Vandanam Scheme: - Andhra Pradesh's initiative provides ₹13,000 per student to mothers of 42.69 lakh school-going children, aiming to support education costs and reduce dropout rates. - Child Abuse and Exploitation - Crime Rates Image: - - Child Marriage: - Driven by poverty and patriarchal norms, child marriage forces early pregnancies and limits education, perpetuating the poverty cycle. - Crimes Against Children: - According to the NCRB 2022, 18 crimes against children occur every hour, with one-third under the POCSO Act. Family members or acquaintances are often perpetrators. - Child Labor: - India has the largest number of working children globally, with over 10 million children aged 5-14 involved in child labor. - Health and Nutrition - Malnutrition: - India faces a high rate of malnutrition, with 32.1% underweight, 35.5% stunted, and 19.3% wasted children. This, along with poor early childhood education, exacerbates learning difficulties. - Mortality and Health Indicators: - India has one of the highest Infant Mortality Rates (IMR) at 34 per 1,000 live births. Diarrhea and malnutrition are primary causes of death for children under five. - Health Comparison Image: - - Best Practices: - Rajiv Aarogyasri Scheme - Telangana has extended health insurance coverage to 2,215 orphans and BPL children in Child Care Institutions, offering cashless treatment up to ₹10 lakh for over 1,835 procedures. - ICDS and Bharat Ratna Dr APJ Abdul Kalam Amrut Aahar Yojana: - Maharashtra reported a 51,141 decrease in severely malnourished children from 2023 to 2025, attributed to initiatives like ICDS and the 'Bharat Ratna Dr APJ Abdul Kalam Amrut Aahar Yojana'. - Gender Discrimination - Child Marriage and Violence: - Female children face infanticide, neglect of nutritional needs, lack of education, and gender-based violence. 7% of girls aged 15 or younger were married between 2014-2020. - Child Sex Ratio: - The child sex ratio (0-6 years) was 919 girls per 1,000 boys in 2011, reflecting ongoing gender imbalances. - Other Vulnerabilities - Digital Well-Being: - The digital well-being of children in India is a growing concern, with 60% of children at risk of digital addiction. 70-80% of children exceed recommended screen time, leading to issues like cyberbullying, mental health concerns, and behavioral changes. - Digital Safety Image: - - Climate Change Impact: - Children are disproportionately affected by natural disasters and climate change, exacerbating their vulnerabilities. In 2023, approximately 24.1 million children in India were impacted annually by climate-related disasters such as floods, cyclones, and heatwaves. - Side note - Learning through Example- A Special Start- Finland's Baby Box - Finland’s Baby Box policy is a powerful example of how governments can promote equal opportunity from birth. Since 1949, every new mother has received a free "Baby Box" containing essential items for newborn care. - This initiative does more than offer material support. It encourages prenatal care, fosters early child well-being, and has played a role in significantly reducing infant mortality rates in Finland. - Key takeaway: - Social policies that provide universal access to early life resources can have lasting impacts. The Baby Box illustrates how state-led interventions can bridge gaps in healthcare, reduce inequality, and promote a strong, healthy start for every child, regardless of background. - 1.2.2. Government Schemes and Policies for Children - Constitutional Safeguards - Article 39(f): - Ensures children's development in a healthy and dignified environment, protecting them from exploitation. - Article 21A: - Guarantees free and compulsory education for children aged 6-14 years. - Articles 23 & 24: - Prohibit child trafficking and forced labor, with specific restrictions on hazardous labor. - Article 45: - Directs the provision of early childhood care and education for children up to six years. - Welfare and Development Schemes - Integrated Child Development Services (ICDS): - Focuses on the nutritional and health development of children under six. - POSHAN Abhiyaan: - Targets reduction of malnutrition in children. - Samagra Shiksha Abhiyan: - Aims to improve learning outcomes for disadvantaged children, focusing on quality education. - National Education Policy (NEP) 2020: - Prioritizes Early Childhood Care and Education (ECCE) for foundational literacy and numeracy. - Childline-1098 and Mission Vatsalya: - Childline-1098 is a 24-hour emergency helpline for children in distress. Mission Vatsalya has supported 1.7 lakh children under non-institutional care programs in 2024–25, aiming to provide family-based care and rehabilitation. - Protection and Legal Frameworks - Juvenile Justice (Care and Protection of Children) Act, 2015: - Focuses on the care, protection, and rehabilitation of children in need. - Protection of Children from Sexual Offences (POCSO) Act, 2012 and Prohibition of Child Marriage Act, 2006: - POCSO Act provides strict protection against child sexual abuse. Prohibition of Child Marriage Act criminalizes child marriage. - 1.3. Young Adults (Youth) - "The youth are the living messages we send to a future we will never see." - John F. Kennedy - In India, the National Youth Policy 2014 defines youth as individuals between the ages of 15 and 29 years. - Youth is considered a period of transition from dependence to independence, encompassing the age groups of adolescents (10-19 years) and young adults (15-24 years). - With 65% of India's population under 35 years, youth form a critical demographic for national development. - 1.3.1. Key Challenges Faced by Young Adults - Employment Crisis and Skill Mismatch - High Unemployment: - Youth unemployment remains high, with a significant portion of the youth population either underemployed or disengaged from both employment and learning. Youth unemployment Rate: 17.9% in Urban areas and 13.7% in Rural regions as of May 2025. - Skill Gap: - A substantial mismatch exists between available talent and the needs of the industry. Only 4.4% of the workforce aged 15-29 has received formal skills training, contributing to a high unemployment rate. - Informalization: - An increasing trend toward informal, short-term contracts in the job market pushes youth into the informal economy, where social security benefits are absent. - Exploitation under Gig Economy: - Precarious working conditions in the gig economy leads to exploitation of youth. Key factors for such conditions include poor pay, lack of social security, and absence of worker protection regulations. - Mental Health Crisis, Drug Abuse and Suicide Epidemic - Addiction Image: - - Drug Abuse: - Around 13% of drug users in India are under 20 years old, exacerbating physical and mental health issues. - Mental Health Issues: - A growing crisis of mental health problems, including depression and suicidal tendencies, especially among LGBTQIA+ youth, calls for urgent attention. - Suicide Epidemic: - Student suicides highlight the immense social and psychological pressures faced by youth mainly due to mental health issues, social pressures as well as drug abuse. India reported 13,044 student suicides in 2022 (NCRB data), nearly doubling in a decade. - Side note - Learning through Example- Switzerland's Secret to Youth Success - Switzerland offers a strong example of how practical education can prepare youth for meaningful careers. Around 70% of Swiss teenagers choose vocational apprenticeships after school rather than traditional academic routes. - These apprenticeships combine classroom learning with on-the-job training. Companies like Nestlé pay students as they work and learn, ensuring they gain real-world skills and income at the same time. As a result, Switzerland enjoys one of the lowest youth unemployment rates in the world. - Key takeaway: - A well-designed vocational training system equips youth with job-ready skills, reduces unemployment, and bridges the gap between education and industry. It highlights the importance of aligning education with practical experience and market needs. - 1.3.2. Government Schemes and Policies for Youth - Youth Development and Engagement - National Youth Policy 2014: - Aims for holistic youth development to empower youth for nation-building. - Nehru Yuva Kendra Sangathan: - Provides rural youth with opportunities for nation-building and skill development. - National Service Scheme (NSS) and Life Skill Training for Adolescents: - NSS promotes volunteerism and social service. Life Skill Training equips youth with skills to cope with pressures and make healthy life choices. - Mera Yuva Bharat (MY Bharat): - A platform launched in 2023 to engage youth in volunteerism and nation-building activities. - Skill Development and Employment Generation - National Skill Development Mission (NSDM) and Pradhan Mantri Kaushal Vikas Yojana (PMKVY): - NSDM provides a framework to coordinate skill development efforts. PMKVY provides skill training to improve youth employability. - Employment-Linked Incentive (ELI) Scheme: - The Indian government approved an ₹1 trillion Employment-Linked Incentive (ELI) scheme, aiming to create approximately 35 million jobs between August 2025 and July 2027. - Start-Up India and Stand Up India: - Facilitates entrepreneurship by providing financial support to youth entrepreneurs, especially from SC/ST backgrounds. - Social Security and Health - Code on Social Security (2020): - Expands social security benefits to include gig and platform workers, where majority of those employed are youth. - Pradhan Mantri Jeevan Jyoti Bima Yojana (PMJJBY) and Pradhan Mantri Suraksha Bima Yojana (PMSBY): - Affordable insurance schemes for underprivileged youth. - Mental Health Care Act (2017): - Aims to reduce stigmatization and improve access to mental health care for youth. - National Youth Policy (in detail) - - The National Youth Policy (NYP) provides a structured approach to youth development in India. The last finalized version, NYP 2014, focused on empowering individuals aged 15–29 to achieve their full potential. - It aimed to align youth energy with the goals of national development through five key priority areas: education, employment and entrepreneurship, health and sports, youth leadership, and social justice. - The policy emphasized inclusion, skill-building, and active participation in civic life, and was aligned with national policies like NEP 2020 and international frameworks such as the Sustainable Development Goals. - Over time, the need for an updated framework became clear due to changing economic realities, rapid technological shifts, and rising mental health concerns among youth. - Responding to this, the government released the Draft National Youth Policy 2024, building on past learnings and aiming for deeper impact during the Amrit Kaal period. - Key Features Draft National Youth Policy 2024: - Tackling NEET Crisis: - Aims to reduce the NEET (Not in Education, Employment or Training) rate, which is 32.9% of youth aged 15–29 (NSSO 2020–21). - Focus on Youth Demographic: - Targets individuals aged 15–29 years, who constitute India’s largest working-age population segment. - Skill, Up-skill, Re-skill Model: - Emphasizes readiness for Industry 4.0 through continuous skill enhancement aligned with market needs. - Digital Empowerment via YUVA Portal: - Introduces a one-stop online platform for internships, job opportunities, mentoring, and career counseling. - Entrepreneurship and Job Creation: - Encourages youth to become job creators by promoting innovation, startup ecosystems, and e-commerce access. - Leadership Development: - Proposes a School of Leadership at RGNIYD and platforms like Youth Parliament Festival to shape future leaders. - Inclusion of Vulnerable Groups: - Prioritizes outreach to dropouts, rural, disabled, and disadvantaged youth through tailored programs. - Mental Health and Well-being: - Integrates counseling, stress management, and awareness around substance abuse into youth health strategy. - Fitness as Daily Culture: - Promotes “Fitness ki Dose, Aadha Ghanta Roz” and strengthens youth engagement in sports and adventure activities. - Data-Driven Governance: - Recommends District Youth Information & Resource Centers and analytics for real-time youth tracking and support. - The draft aims to create a youth ecosystem that not only supports individuals but positions them as leaders of change in a rapidly evolving India. - 1.4. Elderly (Senior Citizens) in India - "A society that abandons its elders cuts its own roots." - Confucius - In India, "elderly" refers to citizens aged 60 and above. According to the 2011 Census, there were nearly 104 million senior citizens, constituting 8.6% of the population. - This number is projected to triple by 2050, reaching 300 million (18%), with a rising proportion of elderly women, particularly in rural areas, facing issues like poverty, dependency, and loneliness. - - - 1.4.1. Key Challenges Faced by the Elderly - Health Concerns - Chronic Illnesses: - High prevalence of chronic illnesses like diabetes, heart diseases, and cataracts. - Limited Geriatric Care: - Limited access to geriatric care, with few healthcare providers trained for elderly-specific health needs. - Rising Treatment Costs: - Rising treatment costs for conditions like cancer, leading families into financial distress. - Mental Health Issues: - Mental health issues, including depression, loneliness, and identity crises, are common, as they face emotional neglect. Approximately 18% of elderly individuals experience depression, with loneliness affecting about 35% of seniors, particularly in urban areas. - Social Challenges - Changing Family Structures: - The rise of nuclear families has led to a lack of companionship and caregiving. Many elderly thus suffer from social isolation, especially those living alone. A study indicates that 34% of seniors experience social isolation, with 55.4% reporting feelings of loneliness. - Elder Abuse: - Elder abuse is rampant, with incidents of physical, verbal, and emotional abuse, particularly by family members. Elder abuse remains a significant issue, with prevalence rates varying from 9.6% to 61.7% across different states. - Economic Challenges - Limited Pension Coverage: - Only 10% of India's workforce has access to pensions. Even through government schemes, the amount paid is a meagre Rs 200-500 per month, which is insufficient to meet basic needs. - Dependence on Children: - Due to limited pension access, many elderly individuals rely on their children for financial support. - Lack of Health Insurance: - A large portion of elderly individuals work in the informal sector, leaving them without health insurance. This leads to high medical costs and inadequate healthcare. - Housing Challenges: - Many elderly individuals struggle to access adequate housing due to limited financial resources. - Challenges Faced by Elderly Women - Widowhood: - Widowhood is more prevalent among elderly women, leading to increased financial vulnerability. - Gender-Based Discrimination: - Older women are more likely to face abuse and denied property rights due to gender discrimination and traditional societal norms. - Technology Barriers - Digital Literacy Challenges: - Many elderly face challenges with digital literacy, hindering their ability to access services that are increasingly digitalized. - Aadhaar Exclusions: - Elderly individuals' fingerprint degradation can lead to exclusion from welfare schemes that require Aadhaar verification. - Side note - Learning through Example- The Graying Globe - As the world sees a rapid rise in the elderly population, Japan’s experience offers valuable lessons. Often called a “Silver Democracy”, Japan’s political landscape is increasingly shaped by its ageing voters, with policy focus shifting toward pensions, healthcare, and elderly welfare. - One major issue Japan faces is the growing burden of elderly care. To tackle this, it has turned to technological solutions, such as the Robear Care Robot, which assists caregivers and showcases how innovation can ease care responsibilities. - Key takeaway: - Preparing for an aging society requires more than just policy—it demands foresight, welfare planning, and the use of technology. Japan’s approach reminds us that ensuring dignity, inclusion, and support for the elderly is not optional, but essential for social justice in aging democracies. - 1.4.2. Government Schemes and Policies for the Elderly - Constitutional and Legal Framework - Article 41: - Ensures the right to public assistance for old age. - Maintenance and Welfare of Parents and Senior Citizens Act, 2007 (Amended 2019): - Mandates children to provide maintenance to elderly parents and ensures protection of life and property. - Welfare Schemes for the Elderly - Indira Gandhi National Old Age Pension Scheme (IGNOAPS): - Provides monthly pensions to BPL elderly citizens. - Pradhan Mantri Vaya Vandana Yojana (PMVVY): - Ensures a pension for senior citizens with an 8% return on lump sum investments. - National Programme for the Health Care for the Elderly (NPHCE): - Provides preventive and curative healthcare for the elderly. - Rashtriya Vayoshri Yojana (RVY): - Distributes assistive devices (e.g., wheelchairs, hearing aids) to BPL senior citizens. - Senior Citizens Health Insurance Scheme (SCHIS): - Offers health insurance coverage to elderly people in BPL families. - Specific Government Policies and Schemes - National Policy for Senior Citizens, 2011 and Elder Line: - National Policy focuses on welfare, promoting preventive care, income security, and social participation. Elder Line provides a helpline for senior citizens in distress. - Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (AB-PMJAY): - Extended to include all citizens aged 70 and above, covering healthcare costs up to ₹5 lakh. - The Second Demographic Dividend - The second demographic dividend occurs when a large working-age population enters their highest income-earning years, typically between 40-49 years. During this period, individuals focus more on saving for retirement and are at their most productive, contributing to economic growth. - India’s Case: - India should focus on the second demographic dividend by addressing challenges like skill deficits (NEP 2020's goal to improve GER), employment issues (underemployment, especially in the informal sector), and promoting savings, investment, and social security to support future growth. Empowering women and managing urbanization challenges are also critical. - Maximizing Benefits from India's Second Demographic Dividend
S.No.StrategyExample
1Investing in Human CapitalSouth Korea fueled growth by investing in universal education and vocational training, creating a skilled workforce.
2Generating Productive EmploymentChina absorbed rural workers into urban industrial jobs using SEZs and export-oriented policies during its demographic dividend peak.
3Promoting Savings and InvestmentSingapore mobilized savings through CPF, supporting infrastructure development and economic diversification.
4Strengthening Social Security and Healthcare SystemsJapan supported its aging population with universal healthcare and pension systems.
5Empowering WomenVietnam boosted female labor force participation through investments in education, childcare, and gender equality policies.
- 1.5. Scheduled Castes (SCs) in India - "The caste system is like an elevator that stopped working centuries ago - some are trapped in the basement while others enjoy the penthouse." - Dr. B.R. Ambedkar (Reservation policy debates) - Scheduled Castes (SCs) are communities identified under Article 341 of the Indian Constitution as facing historical discrimination and deserving special protection. Initially, this status applied to marginalized Hindu communities, later it was expanded to include Sikhs (1956) and Buddhists (1990). The term Dalit is often used to refer to this group. - - 1.5.1. Key Challenges Faced by Scheduled Castes - Social Exclusion - Social Ostracism: - SCs face social ostracism due to the entrenched caste system. Discrimination persists in public spaces, restricting access to resources, including basic necessities. - Discriminatory Practices: - Social practices like honour killing, female foeticide, child marriage, and manual scavenging continue to affect them. - Economic Disadvantage - Poverty: - As per Census 2011, 34% of SCs lived below the poverty line, much higher than the national average of 9%. - Landlessness: - Approximately 45% of SC households are landless. - Wealth Distribution: - SCs own only 7% of India’s wealth, despite comprising 25.2% of the population. - Informal Sector Employment: - Many SCs work in low-skilled, informal sectors, facing job insecurity and earning lower wages than other castes. - Political Underrepresentation - Limited Representation: - Despite constitutional provisions for reservations, SCs remain underrepresented in decision-making bodies, holding only 4% of senior positions in the government. - Vulnerability and Exploitation - Anti-Dalit Crimes Image: - - Violence and Human Rights Violations: - SCs are disproportionately affected by violence and human rights violations. In 2022, 57,582 crimes were reported against SCs. - Manual Scavenging: - Manual scavenging, primarily undertaken by Dalit women, remains a practice of exploitation. - Overrepresentation in Prisons: - SCs are overrepresented in prisons, constituting 20.74% of the prison population. - Access to Basic Services - Education: - Malnutrition and lack of early childhood education hamper SC children’s learning. - Health: - IMR among SCs is 83 per 1,000 live births, significantly higher than the national average of 61.8. - Basic Amenities: - Only 28% of SCs had access to electricity in 2011, compared to the national average of 48%. - Internal Disparities - Inequalities within SC Communities: - There are significant inequalities within SC communities, with advanced SC sub-groups benefiting more from reservations than marginalized sub-groups. - Manual Scavenging in India- Persistence of a Grave Injustice - - Manual scavenging remains one of India’s most pressing social and human rights challenges, disproportionately impacting Dalits, especially Dalit women. Although outlawed under the Employment of Manual Scavengers and Construction of Dry Latrines (Prohibition) Act, 1993, and further criminalized by the Prohibition of Employment as Manual Scavengers and their Rehabilitation Act, 2013, the practice continues due to a nexus of caste hierarchies, economic deprivation, and poor enforcement. - Scale and Impact - Caste and Gender: - An estimated 98% of manual scavengers are Dalits, and 95% are women (Shah et al., 2015; UN Women, 2014), indicating intersectional marginalisation rooted in caste and patriarchy. - Deaths and Lack of Justice- Over 40 deaths were reported in just six months of 2024 (Safai Karmachari Andolan). - From 1993 to 2020, 1,013 deaths were officially recorded, yet convictions remain rare. - Most cases are labelled as "accidents," and affected families often do not receive compensation or meaningful rehabilitation. - Structural Reasons for Persistence of Manual Scavenging - Entrenched Caste System and social stigma - Lack of alternative livelihoods, education, or land - Poor sanitation infrastructure and slow mechanization - Weak implementation of laws and accountability mechanisms - Apathy from authorities and wider society - Way Forward - Strict enforcement of existing laws with accountability mechanisms - Full mechanization of sanitation work and modern sewage systems - Robust rehabilitation programs, including land, education, and employment - Skill development and financial inclusion for liberated manual scavengers - Societal change through education, media, and sustained public discourse to end caste-based occupational segregation - Sub-Classification of Scheduled Castes (SCs) - The Supreme Court of India, in the State of Punjab & Others v Davinder Singh & Others case, has ruled that sub-classification of Scheduled Castes (SCs) is permissible. - This landmark judgment allows for creating separate quotas for more backward groups within the SC category, addressing specific needs and ensuring better representation in education and employment. - Key Highlights of the Judgment - Constitutional Clarity: - The Supreme Court affirmed that sub-classification within SCs does not violate Article 341(2), as the reservation list is flexible. - State’s Responsibility and Data Collection: - States are allowed to sub-classify SCs based on inadequate representation in public services. However, the state must provide empirical data supporting the claim of backwardness in these groups. - Creamy Layer Principle: - The debate on extending the "creamy layer" concept to SCs and STs was discussed, but the judgment does not mandate its implementation. - Arguments Supporting Sub-Classification of SCs: - Substantive Equality: - The approach ensures the most marginalized within SCs are given targeted support. - Efficient Governance: - Sub-classification can improve governance efficiency by addressing the unique challenges faced by various SC groups. - Recognition of Diversity: - Acknowledges the heterogeneity of SCs, with some groups facing more severe discrimination and social exclusion than others. - Arguments Opposing Sub-Classification of SCs: - Risk of Division: - Critics fear that it could lead to fragmentation within the SC community, weakening their collective voice in socio-political matters. - Potential for Political Exploitation: - There are concerns about the manipulation of sub-classification for vote bank politics. - Data Limitations: - Lack of accurate caste data makes it difficult to substantiate claims of underrepresentation within specific SC subgroups. - The Supreme Court’s ruling on sub-classification marks a significant shift in India’s reservation policies for SCs. However, the government has clarified on multiple occasions that subclassification shall not be done for SCs. - - Learning through Example- Japan - Dowa Education Movement - Japan’s Burakumin community, once treated as social outcasts, faced generations of systemic discrimination. In response, they initiated the Dowa Education Movement, using education as a transformative tool. - Schools were encouraged to teach about Burakumin history and human rights, aiming to challenge prejudice early in life. By raising awareness among children, this approach helped shift mindsets and promote dignity and equality. - Key takeaway: - Long-term social change often begins in the classroom. Curriculum reform and inclusive education are powerful means to challenge stereotypes and build a more equitable society from the ground up. - 1.5.2. Government Schemes and Policies for Scheduled Castes - Constitutional and Legal Safeguards - Article 14: - Ensures equality before the law for SCs. - Article 15(4) & 15(5): - Enables the State to make provisions for the advancement of SCs in education and public services. - Article 17: - Abolishes untouchability and prohibits its practice in any form. - Article 46: - Directs the State to promote the welfare and protect SCs from social injustice and exploitation. - Key Acts for Protection - Protection of Civil Rights Act, 1955: - Legal safeguards against caste-based discrimination. - Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes (Prevention of Atrocities) Act, 1989: - Prevents atrocities and provides financial assistance to victims. - Prohibition of Employment as Manual Scavengers and their Rehabilitation Act, 2013: - Bans manual scavenging and mandates rehabilitation. - Government Initiatives and Schemes - SC Sub-Plan (SCSP): - Allocates funds specifically for the development of SCs across various sectors. - Post-Matric Scholarships: - Financial assistance for SC students pursuing higher education. - Mahila Adhikarita Yojana: - Financial assistance for SC women to promote income generation. - National Safai Karamchari Finance and Development Corporation (NSKFDC): - Provides financial assistance for income-generating schemes for SCs. - Health and Social Welfare - NAMASTE Scheme: - Aims to train sewer/septic tank workers and provide PPE kits. - Rashtriya Swasthya Bima Yojana (RSBY): - Health insurance component for SCs and other vulnerable groups. - Economic Empowerment - Stand Up India and PM Mudra Yojana: - Stand Up India facilitates bank loans for SC/ST and women entrepreneurs to promote self-employment. PM Mudra Yojana provides collateral-free loans to promote micro-enterprises, including those owned by SCs. - Institutional Support - National Commission for Scheduled Castes (NCSC) and National Commission for Safai Karamcharis (NCSK): - NCSC monitors and safeguards the socio-economic interests of SCs. NCSK focuses on the welfare of manual scavengers. - 1.6. Scheduled Tribes (STs) in India - "The land does not belong to us; we belong to the land." - Gond proverb (Tribal Wisdom) - Scheduled Tribes (STs) in India are social groups identified by the Constitution as suffering from poverty, powerlessness, and social stigma. The Constitution of India defines Scheduled Tribes under Article 366 (25), which designates them for special treatment and ensures their protection and welfare. - According to the 2011 Census, STs constitute approximately 8% of India's total population, with a literacy rate of 60%, lower than the national average of 75%. They also face high levels of poverty, unemployment, and social exclusion. - - - 1.6.1. Key Challenges Faced by Scheduled Tribes - Poverty and Economic Disadvantage - High Poverty Rates: - 45.3% of rural ST populations live below the poverty line, a significantly higher proportion compared to other social groups. - Limited Economic Opportunities: - Many ST households are landless or have limited access to economic opportunities, contributing to their economic disadvantage. - Land Alienation and Resource Depletion - Impact of Development Projects: - Development projects, such as mining and dam constructions, have led to land alienation, depriving STs of their traditional land and natural resources, thereby impacting their livelihood and culture. - Educational and Health Disadvantages - Poor Health and Education Outcomes: - STs face poor health outcomes, including higher infant mortality rates (IMR), under-five mortality rates (U5MR), and high levels of anemia among women. They also suffer from low literacy rates (59% in 2011) and high dropout rates, hindering educational progress. - Crime and Violence - Rising Hate Crimes Image: - - Violence Against Tribal Women: - Tribal communities, particularly tribal women, are disproportionately affected by violence. In 2022, there were 1,347 rape cases and 1,022 assault cases reported against tribal women, highlighting their vulnerability and lack of protection. - How the Forest Rights Act (FRA) Gives Power Back to Forest-Dwelling Tribes - For generations, STs living in forests faced injustice, with governments taking away their land and rights. The Forest Rights Act (FRA), passed in 2006, aims to correct these wrongs and give STs and other traditional forest dwellers legal control over their ancestral forests. - 1. Fixing Past Injustices- Recognizing Land & Resource Rights - Legal Ownership: - The FRA gives STs legal recognition of their rights over the forest land they’ve lived on for generations. - Types of Rights Granted: - Individual Forest Rights (IFR): - The right for families to live on and use forest land for their livelihood. - Community Forest Rights (CFR): - The right for entire villages to manage common resources like grazing land, water sources, and Minor Forest Produce (MFP) such as bamboo and herbs. - Community Forest Resource Rights (CFRR): - The right and responsibility to protect, regenerate, and manage local forests sustainably. - 2. Empowering Tribal Communities- Putting Decisions in Local Hands - Gram Sabha is Key: - The Gram Sabha (village assembly) has the power to decide on forest rights, giving STs authority over local governance. - Protection from Forced Removal: - The Act protects STs from illegal evictions and ensures proper rehabilitation and resettlement if land is taken for important projects. - Securing Tribal Homelands: - For Particularly Vulnerable Tribal Groups (PVTGs), the FRA secures their rights to their entire traditional habitat, even if some parts are classified as revenue land. - 3. Building Better Livelihoods & Economic Security - Access to Forest Bounty: - Legal rights to collect, use, and sell Minor Forest Produce (MFP) help create sustainable livelihoods and improve food security. - Development Needs Met: - The Act allows the use of small amounts of forest land for building essential community services like schools, health clinics, and roads. - 4. Protecting Culture & Conserving Forests Together - Valuing Traditional Knowledge: - The FRA protects the traditional knowledge of STs about biodiversity and conservation. - Community-Led Conservation: - The Act empowers STs to manage forests sustainably, helping preserve biodiversity, combat climate change, and protect cultural heritage. - Changes to the Act- Steps Forward & Concerns - Positive Change (2017): - Reclassifying bamboo as Minor Forest Produce (MFP) allowed STs to harvest and sell bamboo freely, boosting incomes. - Recent Concerns (2023 Amendments): - Amendments to the Forest Conservation Act (FCAA 2023) raised concerns that changes could weaken the requirement for Gram Sabha consent and FRA compliance before diverting forest land for projects, threatening ST rights. - Implementation Problems: - Despite positive changes, slow and inconsistent FRA implementation across many states remains a challenge. - Particularly Vulnerable Tribal Groups (PVTGs) - - PVTGs are a sub-class of Scheduled Tribes recognized by the Government of India as the most socio-economically marginalized tribal communities. There are currently 75 PVTGs spread across 18 states and the Andaman & Nicobar Islands. - Identification Criteria for PVTGs - Pre-agricultural level of technology - Stagnant or declining population - Extremely low literacy levels - Subsistence-level economy - Key Issues faced by PVTGs - Displacement and Loss of Livelihoods: - Displaced by development, conservation, and infrastructure projects with poor rehabilitation support. - Denial of Land and Habitat Rights: - Despite laws like the Forest Rights Act, many remain alienated from ancestral lands and resources. - Extreme Poverty and Malnutrition: - High levels of hunger, health issues, and literacy rates around only 30–40%. - Exploitation and Market Exclusion: - Limited access to markets for forest produce and vulnerability during health or climate crises. - Data Gaps and Outdated Lists: - Inadequate surveys and demographic data result in ineffective targeting of welfare programs. - Government Initiatives - PM-JANMAN Scheme: - It is a new scheme launched by Government of India, especially focused on saturating PVTG habitations with basic amenities such as: - Housing - Drinking water - Healthcare and nutrition - Road connectivity - Education and skill development - ₹24,000 crore allocated for implementation. - Way Forward - Ensure community-led planning and culturally sensitive development. - Improve infrastructure and access to public services. - Guarantee legal and land rights, with effective implementation of the Forest Rights Act. - Promote livelihood diversification through skill training and forest-based enterprises. - Strengthen data collection and regularize PVTG lists to enable targeted delivery. - - Side note - Learning through Example- First Nations Education Stewardship (Canada) - - In Canada, First Nations communities have reclaimed control over their children’s education through a model called Education Stewardship. - Instead of following externally imposed curricula, tribal councils now design their own content—teaching indigenous languages, traditional skills like hunting, and land-based knowledge. - This model moves beyond colonial systems to build cultural pride, relevance, and resilience. Children learn in ways that affirm their identity, heritage, and community values, fostering both educational engagement and strong self-worth. - Key Takeaway: - When communities shape their own education:
- Learning becomes culturally rooted and contextually meaningful
- It strengthens identity, dignity, and participation
- Bridges trust gaps between learners and institutions
- Empowers the next generation to preserve heritage while progressing - Empowering marginalized groups to steer their educational journey can drive lasting inclusion and development. - 1.6.2. Government Schemes and Policies for Protection and Welfare of STs - Constitutional Provisions - Article 15(5): - Allows the state to make special provisions for the advancement of STs in educational institutions. - Article 16(4): - Permits the reservation of vacancies for STs in public services. - Article 17: - Abolishes untouchability, ensuring STs are not discriminated against. - Article 46: - Directs the State to promote the educational and economic interests of STs. - Article 338A: - Establishes the National Commission for Scheduled Tribes (NCST) to monitor and safeguard STs' rights. - Legal Safeguards - Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes (Prevention of Atrocities) Act, 1989: - Provides legal protection and punitive measures for atrocities committed against STs. - Forest Rights Act, 2006: - Grants STs and other forest-dwelling communities the right to access and manage forest resources. - Welfare Schemes and Educational Initiatives - Kasturba Gandhi Balika Vidyalayas: - Residential schools for tribal girls to improve their education. - Eklavya Model Residential Schools (EMRS): - Provides quality education with a focus on tribal culture and heritage. - Post-Matric Scholarships: - Financial aid for ST students pursuing higher education. - Healthcare Initiatives - National Tribal Health Care Plan: - Focuses on improving healthcare accessibility for tribal populations. - Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana (PMJAY): - Extends health insurance benefits to vulnerable tribal populations. - Economic Empowerment - Mahila Adhikarita Yojana: - Offers financial assistance to ST women for income-generating activities. - Dharti Aaba Janjatiya Gram Utkarsh Abhiyan (DAJGUA): - Initiatives like the Dharti Aaba Janjatiya Gram Utkarsh Abhiyan (DAJGUA), with an outlay of ₹80,000 crore over five years, aim to enhance the socio-economic conditions of tribal communities. - 1.7. Other Backward Classes (OBCs) in India - "An OBC student doesn't need lowered cutoffs - she needs the childhood nutrition you had." - Savitribai Phule Study Circle pamphlet - Other Backward Classes (OBCs) in India refer to a group of communities that are educationally or socially disadvantaged but do not fall under the categories of Scheduled Castes (SCs) or Scheduled Tribes (STs). - Although the term "OBC" is not directly defined in the Constitution, it allows the State to make special provisions for the advancement of these classes. The Mandal Commission report, 1980, recommended identifying backward communities based on certain social and educational criteria, leading to the establishment of a list for OBCs. As per the 2004-05 NSSO round, OBCs make up about 41% of India's population. - 1.7.1. Key Challenges Faced by OBCs - Socioeconomic Disparities - Poverty and Inequality: - A significant proportion of OBCs live below the poverty line, with limited access to essential services like education and healthcare. - Concentration in Rural: - OBCs are often concentrated in rural areas, where they are primarily engaged in agriculture or low-skilled informal jobs, leading to low incomes and limited job security.
